Unitary and Open Scattering Quantum Walks on Graphs
Alain Joye

Abstract
We consider a class of Unitary Quantum Walks on arbitrary graphs, parameterized by a family of scattering matrices. These Scattering Quantum Walks model the discrete dynamics of a system on the graph’s edges, where a scattering process at the vertices is governed by the scattering matrices assigned to each vertex. We show the Scattering Quantum Walks encompass several known Quantum Walks. We further introduce two classes of Scattering Open Quantum Walks on arbitrary graphs, also parameterized by scattering matrices: one defined on the edges, the other on the vertices of the graph. We show these walks give rise to proper quantum channels and describe their main spectral and dynamical properties, relating them to naturally associated classical Markov chains. 1 Introduction Quantum Walks (QWs) defined on graphs or lattices are popular discrete time linear dynamical systems which are used in different scientific fields under different guises and for various applications, see e.g. the books and reviews [ Ke , V-A , P , J4 , ABJ3 , GZ , QMS ] , and references therein. For example, in quantum optics or condensed matter physics, QWs provide a versatile tool to approximate the complex dynamics of certain systems in some physically relevant regimes, [ KFC+ , ZKG+ , CC , WM , TMT ] . QWs are used in the field of quantum computing and information processing, notably as quantum search algorithms, see [ C , Sa , P , KMOR ] . From a probabilistic perspective, QWs are considered as non commutative extensions of classical random walks and Markov chains, see e.g. [ Sz , Ko2 , G , APSS1 ] . The success and diversity of the different types of QWs have sparked the interest of the mathematical community which has rigorously addressed several of their properties, such as the spectral and dynamical properties of certain QWs, [ Ko1 , BHJ , HKSS2 , MS-B , JMa , HS , AG-PS , HSS , RST1 , RST2 , T , RT , CJWW ] , Anderson (de-)localization in various models of random and quasiperiodic QWs [ Kos , J2 , HJS1 , HJS2 , J3 , JMe , ASW , ABJ1 , ABJ2 , HJ1 , BM , CFGW , CFO , CFL+ ] , many-body systems of QWs [ AAM+ , HJ2 , R , AJR ] , or the topological properties of QWs [ ABJ4 , DFT , CGG+ , SS-B , ST , D , ABJ5 ] , to mention a few. In this paper, we introduce and study the notion of Scattering Quantum Walks (SQWs) on arbitrary graphs, a class of QWs broad enough to encompass many QWs present in the literature, and which moreover admits both unitary and open versions. Unitary SQWs are used to address closed quantum systems, while open SQWs are relevant in the analysis of open quantum systems and are known as quantum channels. Let us describe informally the definition of a unitary SQW on a graph G G . It is a unitary operator on a Hilbert space associated with G G , which models the linear dynamics of a quantum system, or quantum walker, over a single time step. For a simple graph G = ( V , E ) G=(V,E) with vertex set V V and undirected edge set E E , we consider that each edge gives rise to two directed edges with opposite directions, and denote by D D the set of directed edges. We attach to each directed edge a canonical basis vector of a Hilbert space l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) . The SQW on l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) is parameterized by a family of scattering matrices, each assigned to a vertex in V V . Informally, we say that the quantum walker lives on the directed edges of the graph, as its quantum state is represented by a normalized vector of l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) . At each time step, the state of the quantum walker on a given edge with a specific direction undergoes a scattering process at the vertex towards which the edge points, say x ∈ V x\in V . This process is governed by the scattering matrix S ⁡ ( x ) S(x) assigned to x x . The walker’s state is then transformed into the corresponding linear combination of states on adjacent edges, with directions now pointing away from the vertex x x where scattering took place, see Figure . x x xxxx S ⁡ ( x ) S(x) ↝ \leadsto x x xxxx S ⁡ ( x ) S(x) Figure 1: The scattering process at work in SQWs The freedom in choosing the directed graph and the unitary scattering matrices at its vertices explains why SQWs encompass a significant range of unitary QWs considered in the literature. We explicitly show that Coined QWs, the Chalker-Coddington model, and a slight generalization of the Grover Walk belong to this class. Following [ HKSS2 , HSS ] we revisit the spectral properties of the latter by relating them to those of a self-adjoint operator defined on l 2 ​ ( V ) l^{2}(V) , the Hilbert space associated the graph’s vertices, through natural boundary operators. We also study SQWs on the star-graph. QWs constructed in a similar way for specific cases have appeared in the physics litterature, see e.g. [ FH1 , FH2 ] . Let us describe informally the construction of an open SQW on the edges of G G now. An open SQW acts on density matrices on l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) representing the states of a quantum walker. We start with an initial state, and measure the position of the quantum walker. This quantum measurement yields a new state depending on the outcome, with a certain quantum mechanical probability. We then evolve this new state using the unitary SQW, resulting in another state. The final state is obtained by taking the expectation value of this state with respect to the quantum mechanical probability distribution of the position observable, which induces decoherence. The open SQW is the map from the initial state to the final state obtained this way. Finally, passing from l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) associated with the edges of G G to l 2 ​ ( V ) l^{2}(V) associated with its vertices by means of boundary operators, which are instrumental in the analysis of the Grover Walk, and after appropriate normalization, we define a second open SQW on the vertices of G G , we call induced by the open SQW on its edges. Our analysis of the dynamical properties of both these open SQWs shows that they are related to those of natural Markov processes defined on the graph, as in [ G , APSS1 ] , which depend on the choice of the scattering matrices. A few examples are worked out to illustrate the richness of these constructions. The paper is organized as follows. Section is devoted to the definition of unitary SQWs on the edges of an arbitrary directed graph, their quantum mechanical interpretation, and their general properties. Particular cases such as the Chalker-Coddington model, Coined Quantum Walks, the star-graph case and the Generalized Grover Walk are discussed in Section . The notions of open SQWs on the edges of a graph and their quantum mechanical interpretation are introduced in Section , while their spectral and dynamical properties are addressed in Section . Finally, Section presents the definition and analysis of induced open SQWs on the vertices of the graph. Both notions of open SQWs are illustrated by examples. An appendix containing technical proofs closes the paper. 2 Unitary Scattering Quantum Walks on a Graph Let us set the notation. A graph G = ( V , E ) G=(V,E) is specified by the set of its vertices V V and the set of its undirected edges E E . The order of the graph G G is | V | |V| , the cardinal of V V , which may be infinite. In the latter case, V V is assumed to be countable and G G is called infinite. For two vertices x ∈ V , y ∈ V x\in V,y\in V forming an edge, we will write x ∼ y x\sim y the fact that [ x ​ y ] ∈ E [xy]\in E . The graphs we consider have no loop, i.e. [ x ​ x ] ∉ E [xx]\not\in E and to avoid trivialities, we will always assume that | V | ≥ 2 |V|\geq 2 and that G G is connected. Each edge gives rise to two directed edges of opposite direction. A directed edge from vertex x x to vertex y y is denoted by ( y ​ x ) (yx) , and the set of directed edges is D D . Finally, the degree of a vertex x ∈ V x\in V , is d x = | { y ∈ V s . t . y ∼ x } | ∈ ℕ ∗ d_{x}=|\{y\in V\ {\rm s.t.}\ y\sim x\}|\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*} , and we have ∑ x ∈ V d x = 2 ​ | E | = | D | \sum_{x\in V}d_{x}=2|E|=|D| . If G G is infinite, we further assume that sup x ∈ V d x = d ¯ < ∞ \sup_{x\in V}d_{x}=\bar{d}<\infty . Furthermore, we pick a vertex r ∈ V r\in V we call the root and for any x ∈ V x\in V , we denote by | x | |x| the graph distance between x x and r r . For any n ∈ ℕ n\in{\mathbb{N}} , the set B n = { x ∈ V s.t. | x | ≤ n } B_{n}=\{x\in V\ \text{s.t.}\ |x|\leq n\} has finite cardinal bounded above by c ​ ( d ¯ − 1 ) n − 1 c(\bar{d}-1)^{n-1} for n n large, d ¯ > 2 \bar{d}>2 , and c c a d ¯ − \bar{d}- dependent constant. We associate to G G the Hilbert space l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) as follows. To each directed edge ( y ​ x ) (yx) from vertex x x to vertex y y , we associate a canonical basis vector of l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) , denoted by | y ​ x ⟩ |yx\rangle , so that, for a finite graph, l 2 ​ ( D ) = span ​ { | y ​ x ⟩ } x , y ∈ V x ∼ y l^{2}(D)=\mbox{\rm span}\{|yx\rangle\}_{x,y\in V\atop x\sim y} , with dim l 2 ​ ( D ) = ∑ x ∈ V d x < ∞ \dim l^{2}(D)=\sum_{x\in V}d_{x}<\infty . In case G G is infinite with countably many vertices, we set l 2 ( D ) = { ψ = ∑ x ∈ V ∑ y ∼ x ψ x ​ y | x y ⟩ , ψ x ​ y ∈ ℂ | ∑ x ∈ V ∑ y ∼ x | ψ x ​ y | 2 < ∞ } . l^{2}(D)=\bigg\{\psi=\sum_{x\in V}\sum_{y\sim x}\psi_{xy}|xy\rangle,\,\psi_{xy}\in{\mathbb{C}}\ \Big|\ \sum_{x\in V}\sum_{y\sim x}|\psi_{xy}|^{2}<\infty\bigg\}. Quantum mechanical interpretation: The quantum system at hand, or quantum walker, has configuration space given by the directed edges ( x ​ y ) (xy) of the graph G G , giving rise to the canonical basis of the Hilbert space l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) . In a state described by a normalized vector ψ ∈ l 2 ​ ( D ) \psi\in l^{2}(D) , the probability of the quantum walker to be on the directed edge ( x ​ y ) (xy) of the graph G G is | ⟨ x ​ y | ψ ⟩ | 2 = | ψ x ​ y | 2 |\langle xy|\psi\rangle|^{2}=|\psi_{xy}|^{2} . The operator U 𝒮 U_{\cal S} defines the one time step evolution of the quantum system. By construction, the state | x ​ y ⟩ |xy\rangle of a quantum walker incoming at the vertex x ∈ V x\in V along the edge ( x ​ y ) (xy) undergoes a local scattering process monitored by the unitary matrix S ⁡ ( x ) S(x) which sends it to a linear combination of outgoing states along the edges ( z ​ x ) (zx) , according to ( ). This local scattering point of view on the quantum dynamics is at work in several physically motivated specific models that, as we will show, are special cases of our definition ( ). The questions of interest concern the determination of the spectrum of U 𝒮 U_{\cal S} and of the related properties of the discrete time dynamical system ( U 𝒮 n ) n ∈ ℤ (U_{\cal S}^{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}} on l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) , as a function of the characteristics of G G , and of the set 𝒮 = { S ⁡ ( x ) } x ∈ V {\cal S}=\{S(x)\}_{x\in V} that parametrize the quantum evolution. The behaviour in time of the probability distribution ℙ n ψ 0 ​ ( ⋅ ) {\mathbb{P}}_{n}^{\psi_{0}}(\cdot) on the set of directed edges { ( x ​ y ) } x ∼ y \{(xy)\}_{x\sim y} induced by the quantum dynamics is also of interest: let ψ 0 ∈ l 2 ​ ( D ) \psi_{0}\in l^{2}(D) be an initial state and ψ n = U 𝒮 n ​ ψ 0 ∈ l 2 ​ ( D ) \psi_{n}=U_{\cal S}^{n}\psi_{0}\in l^{2}(D) be the corresponding state at time n ∈ ℤ n\in{\mathbb{Z}} . For ( x ​ y ) (xy) a directed edge of G G , ℙ n ψ 0 ​ ( ⋅ ) {\mathbb{P}}_{n}^{\psi_{0}}(\cdot) defined by ℙ n ψ 0 ​ ( x ​ y ) = | ⟨ x ​ y | U 𝒮 n ​ ψ 0 ⟩ | 2 = | ⟨ x ​ y | ψ n ⟩ | 2 {\mathbb{P}}_{n}^{\psi_{0}}(xy)=\big|\langle xy|U_{\cal S}^{n}\psi_{0}\rangle\big|^{2}=\big|\langle xy|\psi_{n}\rangle\big|^{2} (2.10) yields the probability to find the quantum walker at time n n on the directed edge ( x ​ y ) (xy) by a measurement of its position. Another important time dependent distribution on the vertices x ∈ V x\in V of the graph, ℚ n ψ 0 ​ ( ⋅ ) {\mathbb{Q}}_{n}^{\psi_{0}}(\cdot) , is the probability to find the quantum walker at time n ∈ ℤ n\in{\mathbb{Z}} in the subspace ℋ x I {\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I} or, improperly, on the vertex x ∈ V x\in V , by a measurement of its position. It is defined by ℚ n ψ 0 ​ ( x ) = ‖ P x I ​ U 𝒮 n ​ ψ 0 ‖ 2 = ‖ P x I ​ ψ n ‖ 2 = ∑ y ∼ x ℙ n ψ 0 ​ ( x ​ y ) . {\mathbb{Q}}_{n}^{\psi_{0}}(x)=\big\|P_{x}^{\rm I}U_{\cal S}^{n}\psi_{0}\big\|^{2}=\big\|P_{x}^{\rm I}\psi_{n}\big\|^{2}=\sum_{y\sim x}{\mathbb{P}}_{n}^{\psi_{0}}(xy). (2.11) 2.1 General Properties We proceed with a few general considerations before discussing some special cases. Perturbation theory: To compare two SQWs defined on the same graph with different sets of scattering matrices, we can resort to the following result which holds in infinite dimension. Such estimates have proven to be instrumental in the analyses of currents in the Chalker-Coddington model, [ ABJ4 , ABJ5 ] , and other network models. Lemma 2.4 For a graph G = ( V , E ) G=(V,E) and two sets of scattering matrices 𝒮 = { S ⁡ ( x ) } x ∈ V {\cal S}=\{S(x)\}_{x\in V} , resp. Defining G ^ = ( V , E ^ ) \widehat{G}=(V,\widehat{E}) as the graph with same set of vertices V V and set of edges E ^ \widehat{E} distinct from E E such that K = ( V , E ∪ E ^ ) K=(V,E\cup\widehat{E}) , i.e. E ^ \widehat{E} is the complement of E E in the set of edges of K K . We will denote that situation as K = G ∪ G ^ K=G\cup\widehat{G} . Accordingly, denoting by l 2 ​ ( D ^ ) l^{2}(\widehat{D}) and l 2 ​ ( D K ) l^{2}(D_{K}) the Hilbert spaces associated with the directed edges of G ^ \widehat{G} and K K , we show that l 2 ​ ( D K ) = l 2 ​ ( D ) ⊕ l 2 ​ ( D ^ ) l^{2}(D_{K})=l^{2}(D)\oplus l^{2}(\widehat{D}) and that U 𝒮 U_{\cal S} can be viewed as the restriction to l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) of a unitary SQW, U 𝒮 K U_{{\cal S}_{K}} , defined on l 2 ​ ( D K ) l^{2}(D_{K}) . Indeed, we note that for any x , y ∈ V x,y\in V , ( x ​ y ) (xy) is a directed edge of K K and is associated with the basis vector | x ​ y ⟩ |xy\rangle of l 2 ​ ( D K ) l^{2}(D_{K}) . Asymptotics of ℚ n ψ 0 ​ ( x ) {\mathbb{Q}}_{n}^{\psi_{0}}(x) : The large n n behaviour of ℚ n ψ 0 ​ ( x ) {\mathbb{Q}}_{n}^{\psi_{0}}(x) , the probability to find the quantum walker on the vertex x ∈ V x\in V at time n n , is oscillatory in case G G is finite. We get a finite limit considering the Cesàro mean, which suppresses the oscillations. In the infinite dimensional case, the Cesàro mean limit involves the nature of σ ⁡ ( U 𝒮 ) \sigma(U_{\cal S}) , according to the RAGE theorem, see e.g. [ Si ] , Theorem 5.5.6, and [ RT ] , Theorems B1 and B2. More precisely, let E ⁡ ( ⋅ ) E(\cdot) defined on ] − π , π ] ]-\pi,\pi] denote the self-adjoint spectral projectors of U 𝒮 U_{\cal S} so that the spectral theorem reads in the resolution of the identity form U 𝒮 = ∫ ] − π , π ] e i ​ θ d E ( θ ) U_{\cal S}=\int_{]-\pi,\pi]}e^{i\theta}dE(\theta) . In particular, E ⁡ ( { θ } ) E(\{\theta\}) is the spectral projector on Ker ⁡ ( U 𝒮 − e i ​ θ ​ 𝕀 ) {\rm Ker\,}(U_{\cal S}-e^{i\theta}{\mathbb{I}}) . See [ ABJ1 , ABJ2 ] for a mathematical approach of this model, and references therein for background. Let { S j , 2 ​ k } j , k ∈ ℤ , with ​ S j , 2 ​ k ∈ U ⁡ ( 2 ) , ∀ j , k , \{{\textsf{S}}_{j,2k}\}_{j,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}},\ \text{with }\ {\textsf{S}}_{j,2k}\in U(2),\ \forall j,k, (3.1) be a collection of scattering matrices, called even or odd according to the parity of j j . The unitary operator which defines the Chalker-Coddington model U C ​ C : l 2 ​ ( ℤ 2 ) → l 2 ​ ( ℤ 2 ) U_{CC}:l^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2})\to l^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2}) (3.2) reads as follows: Let { | j , k ⟩ } j , k ∈ ℤ \{|j,k\rangle\}_{j,k\in{\mathbb{Z}}} be the canonical basis vectors of l 2 ​ ( ℤ 2 ) l^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2}) ; U C ​ C U_{CC} is defined according to figure by: Figure 2: A Chalker–Coddington model with its incoming (black arrows) and outgoing (white arrows) links. (3.3) The convention above is as follows (without indices): ( U ​ | a ⟩ U ​ | b ⟩ ) = S ​ ( | c ⟩ | d ⟩ ) ⇔ { U ⁡ | a ⟩ = S 11 ​ | c ⟩ + S 21 ​ | d ⟩ U ⁡ | b ⟩ = S 12 ​ | c ⟩ + S 22 ​ | d ⟩ , \begin{pmatrix}U|a\rangle\cr U|b\rangle\end{pmatrix}={\textsf{S}}\begin{pmatrix}|c\rangle\cr|d\rangle\end{pmatrix}\Leftrightarrow\begin{cases}U|a\rangle={\textsf{S}}_{11}|c\rangle+{\textsf{S}}_{21}|d\rangle\cr U|b\rangle={\textsf{S}}_{12}|c\rangle+{\textsf{S}}_{22}|d\rangle\end{cases}, (3.4) where S i ​ j {\textsf{S}}_{ij} are the matrix elements of S , in line with ( ). On the grid of Figure , the vertices carrying even matrices S 2 ​ j , 2 ​ k \textsf{S}_{2j,2k} , respectively odd matrices S 2 ​ j + 1 , 2 ​ k \textsf{S}_{2j+1,2k} , have coordinates x e ​ ( j , k ) \displaystyle x^{e}(j,k) = ( 2 j , 2 k ) + ( 1 / 2 , − 1 / 2 ) , respectively \displaystyle=(2j,2k)+(1/2,-1/2),\ \ \text{respectively} x o ​ ( j , k ) \displaystyle x^{o}(j,k) = ( 2 ​ j + 1 , 2 ​ k ) + ( 1 / 2 , 1 / 2 ) , ∀ ( j , k ) ∈ ℤ 2 . \displaystyle=(2j+1,2k)+(1/2,1/2),\ \ \forall(j,k)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}. (3.5) The edges are the diagonal segments labelled by their center ( j , k ) (j,k) on that grid and they have one direction only given by their arrow. Note that we are missing half the directed edges with respect to the construction of l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) , where each edge comes with two orientations. So we associate with each ( j , k ) ∈ ℤ 2 (j,k)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2} the canonical basis vectors | j , k ⟩ − |j,k\rangle_{-} of l 2 ​ ( ℤ 2 ) l^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2}) , corresponding to edges labeled by ( j , k ) (j,k) with orientation opposite to the arrow. These vectors span another copy of l 2 ​ ( ℤ 2 ) l^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2}) we denote by l − 2 ​ ( ℤ 2 ) l^{2}_{-}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2}) . For example, | 2 j , 2 k ⟩ |2j,2k\rangle and | 2 j + 1 , 2 k − 1 ⟩ |2j+1,2k-1\rangle are incoming to x e ​ ( j , k ) x^{e}(j,k) while | 2 j , 2 k ⟩ − |2j,2k\rangle_{-} is incoming to x o ​ ( j − 1 , k ) x^{o}(j-1,k) and | 2 j + 1 , 2 k − 1 ⟩ − |2j+1,2k-1\rangle_{-} is incoming to x o ​ ( j , k − 1 ) x^{o}(j,k-1) , and similarly for the other directed edges. Moreover, we have l 2 ​ ( D ) = l 2 ​ ( ℤ 2 ) ⊕ l − 2 ​ ( ℤ 2 ) l^{2}(D)=l^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2})\oplus l^{2}_{-}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2}) . Since the degree of all vertices is 4, we need 4 × 4 4\times 4 scattering matrices at each vertex to realize the Chalker Coddington model as a SQW on l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) . We label and order the canonical basis of ℂ 4 {\mathbb{C}}^{4} at each vertex as { e N ​ W , e S ​ E , e N ​ E , e S ​ W } \{e_{NW},e_{SE},e_{NE},e_{SW}\} , where the cardinal symbols indicate the incoming and outgoing directions at the vertices, see ( ). Then for every vertex x # ​ ( j , k ) x^{\#}(j,k) , ( j , k ) ∈ ℤ 2 (j,k)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2} , # ∈ { e , o } \#\in\{e,o\} , we define with 𝕆 {\mathbb{O}} the zero 2 × 2 2\times 2 matrix S ⁡ ( x # ​ ( j , k ) ) = ( 𝕆 S 2 ​ j + 1 , 2 ​ k S 2 ​ j , 2 ​ k 𝕆 ) ∈ U ⁡ ( 4 ) S(x^{\#}(j,k))=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbb{O}}&{\textsf{S}}_{2j+1,2k}\cr{\textsf{S}}_{2j,2k}&{\mathbb{O}}\end{pmatrix}\in U(4) (3.6) with respect to the chosen ordered basis, and set 𝒮 C ​ C = { S ⁡ ( x # ​ ( j , k ) ) } ( j , k ) ∈ ℤ 2 # ∈ { e , o } {\cal S}_{CC}=\{S(x^{\#}(j,k))\}_{(j,k)\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}\atop\#\in\{e,o\}} . Remark 3.2 Casting the Chalker-Coddington model into a SQW amounts to add another independent copy of the model in the process of completing the set of directed edges. Proof : It is a matter of computation to check that the action of U 𝒮 C ​ C U_{{\cal S}_{CC}} on basis vectors of the form | j , k ⟩ |j,k\rangle coincides with ( 3.3 ) while its action on vectors | j , k ⟩ − |j,k\rangle_{-} yields ( U ℤ 2 𝒮 C ​ C | 2 j + 1 , 2 k ⟩ − U ℤ 2 𝒮 C ​ C | 2 j , 2 k − 1 ⟩ − ) = S 2 ​ j + 1 , 2 ​ k ​ ( | 2 j , 2 k ⟩ − | 2 j + 1 , 2 k − 1 ⟩ − ) , \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}U_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}^{{\cal S}_{CC}}|2j+1,2k\rangle_{-}\cr U_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}^{{\cal S}_{CC}}|2j,2k-1\rangle_{-}\end{pmatrix}={\textsf{S}}_{2j+1,2k}\begin{pmatrix}|2j,2k\rangle_{-}\cr|2j+1,2k-1\rangle_{-}\end{pmatrix}, ( U ℤ 2 𝒮 C ​ C | 2 j + 1 , 2 k + 1 ⟩ − U ℤ 2 𝒮 C ​ C | 2 j + 2 , 2 k ⟩ − ) = S 2 ​ j , 2 ​ k ​ ( | 2 j + 2 , 2 k + 1 ⟩ − | 2 j + 1 , 2 k ⟩ − ) . \displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}U_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}^{{\cal S}_{CC}}|2j+1,2k+1\rangle_{-}\cr U_{{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}}^{{\cal S}_{CC}}|2j+2,2k\rangle_{-}\end{pmatrix}={\textsf{S}}_{2j,2k}\begin{pmatrix}|2j+2,2k+1\rangle_{-}\cr|2j+1,2k\rangle_{-}\end{pmatrix}. (3.8) Therefore both l 2 ​ ( ℤ 2 ) l^{2}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2}) and l − 2 ​ ( ℤ 2 ) l^{2}_{-}({\mathbb{Z}}^{2}) are invariant under U 𝒮 C ​ C U_{{\cal S}_{CC}} and, by inspection, U ~ C ​ C \tilde{U}_{CC} coincides with a Chalker-Coddington model as described. □ \Box The Chalker-Coddington model is one of many quantum network models. While this designation is not universal, it often refers to QWs defined on graphs in a similar fashion as ( ) with the understanding that the edges can be travelled by the quantum walker in one direction only. This imposes the degrees d x d_{x} to be even for all x ∈ V x\in V , so that the scattering matrix S ⁡ ( x ) ∈ U ⁡ ( d x ) S(x)\in U(d_{x}) assigned to x ∈ V x\in V maps the d x / 2 d_{x}/2 incoming edges at x x to the d x / 2 d_{x}/2 outgoing edges from x x , see [ D ] for example. The Scattering Zippers, introduced and studied in [ MS-B , BM ] provide another example related to the Chalker-Coddington model defined on a strip. Without going into the details, such quantum network models can be viewed as restrictions of SQW defined on a doubled Hilbert space, as we saw for the Chalker-Model model. 3.2 Coined Quantum Walks Coined QWs defined on graphs as introduced by [ AAKV ] , provide a useful concept in quantum computing and a versatile modeling tool in quantum dynamics. We show that they also belong to the class of SQWs on regular graphs. Let us consider the case where all scattering matrices are identity d x × d x d_{x}\times d_{x} matrices, S ⁡ ( x ) = 𝕀 d x S(x)={\mathbb{I}}_{d_{x}} , which reduces ( ) to F = ∑ x ∈ V ∑ y ∼ x | y ​ x ⟩ ​ ⟨ x ​ y | = F ∗ = F − 1 . F=\sum_{x\in V}\sum_{y\sim x}|yx\rangle\langle xy|=F^{*}=F^{-1}. (3.9) The notation F F is justified by the flip property F ​ | x ​ y ⟩ = | y ​ x ⟩ F|xy\rangle=|yx\rangle , for all x ∼ y x\sim y . Therefore F F is reduced by all orthogonal two-dimension subspaces ℋ x ​ y = span ​ { | x ​ y ⟩ , | y ​ x ⟩ } {\cal H}_{xy}=\mbox{span }\{|xy\rangle,|yx\rangle\} , where x ∼ y x\sim y , with matrix representation in the ordered basis { | x ​ y ⟩ , | y ​ x ⟩ } \{|xy\rangle,|yx\rangle\} , F | ℋ x ​ y = ( 0 1 1 0 ) ⇒ F = ⨁ ( x ​ y ) ∈ E F | ℋ x ​ y ​ and ​ σ ​ ( F ) = { − 1 , 1 } . F|_{{\cal H}_{xy}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\cr 1&0\end{pmatrix}\ \Rightarrow\ F=\bigoplus_{(xy)\in E}F|_{{\cal H}_{xy}}\ \mbox{and}\ \sigma(F)=\{-1,1\}. (3.10) This trivial case allows for the decomposition of the general case according to U 𝒮 = F ⁡ ( F ​ U 𝒮 ) where F | ℋ x O : ℋ x O → ℋ x I , and F ​ U 𝒮 | ℋ x I : ℋ x I → ℋ x I , ∀ x ∈ V , U_{\cal S}=F(FU_{\cal S})\ \ \mbox{where}\ \ F|_{{\cal H}_{x}^{\rm O}}:{\cal H}_{x}^{\rm O}\rightarrow{\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I},\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ FU_{\cal S}|_{{\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I}}:{\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I}\rightarrow{\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I},\ \ \forall x\in V, (3.11) such that F ​ U 𝒮 = ⨁ x ∈ V S ⁡ ( x ) where S ⁡ ( x ) ≃ ∑ y ∼ x z ∼ x S z ​ y ​ ( x ) ​ | x ​ z ⟩ ​ ⟨ x ​ y | : ℋ x I → ℋ x I . FU_{\cal S}=\bigoplus_{x\in V}S(x)\ \ \mbox{where}\ \ S(x)\simeq\sum_{y\sim x\atop z\sim x}S_{zy}(x)|xz\rangle\langle xy|:{\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I}\rightarrow{\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I}. (3.12) In the decomposition ( ), F F is responsible for the motion of the quantum walker between different subspaces ℋ x I {\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I} , while F ​ U 𝒮 FU_{\cal S} changes its state locally within each ℋ x I {\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I} . The general structure ( ) of U 𝒮 U_{\cal S} as the composition of a local unitary operator and an operator that implements the motion is that of a Coined QW. However, in many models of quantum dynamics by Coined QWs on infinite regular graphs derived from the Schrödinger equation, the operator responsible for the motion of the walker is unitarily equivalent to a direct sum of shifts, analogous to the effect of the Laplacian, and therefore has absolutely continuous spectrum. The effect of the potential is local and analogous to the action of F ​ U 𝒮 FU_{\cal S} . These specificities are relevant when analyzing the spectral and dynamical (de-)localization properties of Random QWs, see e.g. [ Kos , J3 , HJ1 , JMe , ABJ2 , ABJ3 , ABJ4 , ABJ5 ] . Coined QWs of this sort defined on regular infinite graphs can be viewed as SQW: Assume G G is an infinite d d regular graph, and let θ : { 1 , 2 , … , d } \theta:\{1,2,\dots,d\} be a permutation. Each vertex x ∈ V x\in V forms exactly d d edges with vertices in V V that are labeled x 1 , x 2 , … , x d x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d} . Set F θ F_{\theta} , the operator defined by its action on the basis vectors of l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) F θ | ℋ x I : ℋ x I → ℋ x O s.t. F θ ​ | x ​ x j ⟩ = | x θ ⁡ ( j ) ​ x ⟩ , ∀ j ∈ { 1 , 2 , … , d } , x ∈ V . F_{\theta}|_{{\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I}}:{\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I}\rightarrow{\cal H}_{x}^{\rm O}\ \ \text{s.t.}\ \ F_{\theta}|xx_{j}\rangle=|x_{\theta(j)}x\rangle,\ \ \forall\ j\in\{1,2,\dots,d\},x\in V. (3.13) By definition, F θ F_{\theta} is unitary, as a change of basis, and its adjoint F θ ∗ F_{\theta}^{*} acts as F θ ∗ | ℋ x O : ℋ x O → ℋ x I s.t. F θ ∗ ​ | x j ​ x ⟩ = | x ​ x θ − 1 ​ ( j ) ⟩ , ∀ j ∈ { 1 , 2 , … , d } , x ∈ V . In this section, we introduce and analyze a slight generalization of the Grover walk and we prove a spectral mapping theorem between this QW and a self-adjoint operator related to the adjacency matrix of the graph G G . This kind of result has been shown in various setups in a series of papers, see [ HKSS2 , HS , HSS ] , for the Grover walk, by means of boundary operators. Our approach of the generalized Grover walk based on the Feshbach-Schur method proposes an alternative route to that used in these references, that we believe is interesting in its own right. We provide a first result for finite graphs, that we then generalize to the infinite dimensional case in an abstract setting. Note that the boundary operator method developed in [ HKSS2 , HS , HSS ] to reduce the problem originally stated on l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) to an operator on l 2 ​ ( V ) l^{2}(V) , will be used again in Section to define induced open SQWs. Let G G be finite and let α ∈ ( − π , π ] \alpha\in(-\pi,\pi] . For each x ∈ V x\in V , let ω ⁡ ( x ) ∈ ℂ d x \omega(x)\in{\mathbb{C}}^{d_{x}} such that ‖ ω ⁡ ( x ) ‖ = 1 \|\omega(x)\|=1 . The generalized Grover walk of parameter α \alpha , or α − \alpha- Grover walk, is defined by the set 𝒮 = { S ⁡ ( x ) , x ∈ V } {\cal S}=\{S(x),x\in V\} where: S ⁡ ( x ) = | ω ⁡ ( x ) ⟩ ​ ⟨ ω ⁡ ( x ) | + e i ​ α ​ ( 𝕀 d x − | ω ⁡ ( x ) ⟩ ​ ⟨ ω ⁡ ( x ) | ) , ∀ x ∈ V . S(x)=|\omega(x)\rangle\langle\omega(x)|+e^{i\alpha}({\mathbb{I}}_{d_{x}}-|\omega(x)\rangle\langle\omega(x)|),\ \ \forall x\in V. (3.44) For α = 0 \alpha=0 , S ⁡ ( x ) = 𝕀 d x S(x)={\mathbb{I}}_{d_{x}} , and α = π \alpha=\pi corresponds to the Grover walk. Note that in case d x = 1 d_{x}=1 , S ⁡ ( x ) = 1 S(x)=1 . We thus assume α ≠ 0 \alpha\neq 0 in the following and note that ( ) yields the spectral decomposition of S ⁡ ( x ) S(x) , with σ ⁡ ( S ⁡ ( x ) ) = { 1 , e i ​ α } \sigma(S(x))=\{1,e^{i\alpha}\} , where 1 has multiplicity 1 and e i ​ α e^{i\alpha} has multiplicity 1 − d x 1-d_{x} . The corresponding SQW is denoted by U α U_{\alpha} in this section. (3.45) Accordingly, with F F given in ( ), F ​ U α = ( Π + e i ​ α ​ ( 𝕀 − Π ) ) , FU_{\alpha}=(\Pi+{\rm e}^{i\alpha}({\mathbb{I}}-\Pi)), (3.46) see ( ), where Π = ⨁ x ∈ V | ω ⁡ ( x ) ⟩ ​ ⟨ ω ⁡ ( x ) | = Π 2 = Π ∗ \Pi=\bigoplus_{x\in V}|\omega(x)\rangle\langle\omega(x)|=\Pi^{2}=\Pi^{*} (3.47) is the spectral projector corresponding to the eigenvalue 1 of F ​ U α FU_{\alpha} , and 𝕀 − Π {\mathbb{I}}-\Pi is that corresponding to the eigenvalue e i ​ α e^{i\alpha} of that unitary operator, with dim Π = | V | , and ​ dim ( 𝕀 − Π ) = | D | − | V | . \dim\Pi=|V|,\ \text{and}\ \dim({\mathbb{I}}-\Pi)=|D|-|V|. (3.48) We introduce the orthogonal subspaces ℋ 1 = Π ​ l 2 ​ ( D ) {\cal H}_{1}=\Pi l^{2}(D) , ℋ 2 = ( 𝕀 − Π ) ​ l 2 ​ ( D ) {\cal H}_{2}=({\mathbb{I}}-\Pi)l^{2}(D) and express the operator F F in matrix form according to l 2 ​ ( D ) = ℋ 1 ⊕ ℋ 2 l^{2}(D)={\cal H}_{1}\oplus{\cal H}_{2} as F = ( F 11 F 12 F 21 F 22 ) , where ​ F i ​ j : ℋ j → ℋ i . (3.64) We now turn to a description of the spectral mapping in a more abstract infinite dimensional framework by means of the Feshbach-Schur map. Keeping the same notation and conventions as above, the starting point is again U = F ⁡ ( Π + e i ​ α ​ ( 𝕀 − Π ) ) , with ​ F = F ∗ = F − 1 , e i ​ α ≠ 1 , U=F(\Pi+{\rm e}^{i\alpha}({\mathbb{I}}-\Pi)),\ \text{with}\ F=F^{*}=F^{-1},\ {\rm e}^{i\alpha}\neq 1, (3.65) defined on ℋ = Π ​ ℋ ⊕ ( 𝕀 − Π ) ​ ℋ = ℋ 1 ⊕ ℋ 2 {\cal H}=\Pi{\cal H}\oplus({\mathbb{I}}-\Pi){\cal H}={\cal H}_{1}\oplus{\cal H}_{2} , where Π = Π ∗ = Π 2 ≠ 0 \Pi=\Pi^{*}=\Pi^{2}\neq 0 and dim ℋ = ∞ \dim{\cal H}=\infty is allowed. In block form we have for any z ∈ ℂ z\in{\mathbb{C}} U − z = ( F 11 − z e i ​ α ​ F 12 F 21 e i ​ α ​ F 22 − z ) . The equality of their dimensions is a consequence of the cyclicity of the trace and ( 3.88 ). □ \Box 4 Scattering Open Quantum Walks on a Graph 4.1 Definition and Properties In this section, we define an open QW on a graph G G , denoted Φ 𝒮 \Phi_{{\cal S}} , parameterized by the set of scattering matrices 𝒮 = { S ⁡ ( x ) } x ∈ V {\cal S}=\{S(x)\}_{x\in V} , in the same spirit as the unitary QW U 𝒮 U_{\cal S} . An open QW is a completely positive trace preserving (CPTP) linear map Φ 𝒮 \Phi_{{\cal S}} on 𝒯 ​ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) {\cal T}(l^{2}(D)) , the set of trace class operators acting on l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) , also known as a quantum channels. Let us briefly recall these notions in our framework, referring the reader to the books [ A , AJP , AL , Kr , Sc , Wa ] , for more information. Let ℋ {\cal H} be a separable Hilbert space over ℂ {\mathbb{C}} and ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) {\cal B}({\cal H}) the C ∗ C^{*} algebra of all bounded operators on ℋ {\cal H} equipped with the operator norm. A positive map Φ : ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) → ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) \Phi:{\cal B}({\cal H})\rightarrow{\cal B}({\cal H}) is characterized by Φ ⁡ ( A ) ≥ 0 \Phi(A)\geq 0 for all A ≥ 0 A\geq 0 , A ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) A\in{\cal B}({\cal H}) , while it is called n − n- positive if Φ ⊗ 𝕀 n : ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) ⊗ M n ​ ( ℂ ) → ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) ⊗ M n ​ ( ℂ ) \Phi\otimes{\mathbb{I}}_{n}:{\cal B}({\cal H})\otimes M_{n}({\mathbb{C}})\rightarrow{\cal B}({\cal H})\otimes M_{n}({\mathbb{C}}) is positive, where M n ​ ( ℂ ) M_{n}({\mathbb{C}}) is the set of square complex matrices on ℂ n {\mathbb{C}}^{n} and 𝕀 n {\mathbb{I}}_{n} denotes the identity map on M n ​ ( ℂ ) M_{n}({\mathbb{C}}) . A map Φ \Phi is completely positive (CP), if it is n − n- positive for all n ∈ ℕ n\in{\mathbb{N}} and it is called unital if Φ ⁡ ( 𝕀 ) = 𝕀 \Phi({\mathbb{I}})={\mathbb{I}} . Let 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) ⊂ ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) {\cal T}({\cal H})\subset{\cal B}({\cal H}) be the set of trace class operators on ℋ {\cal H} , which is a Banach space when endowed with the trace norm ∥ ⋅ ∥ 1 \|\cdot\|_{1} . For a map Φ : 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) → 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) \Phi:{\cal T}({\cal H})\rightarrow{\cal T}({\cal H}) , the notions of (complete) positivity are defined as above and such a map is called trace preserving (TP) if tr ​ Φ ​ ( A ) = tr ​ A {\rm tr}\,\Phi(A)={\rm tr}A for all A ∈ 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) A\in{\cal T}({\cal H}) . The set of CPTP maps is convex and the composition of CPTP maps is CPTP. Kraus’s Representation Theorem states that any CPTP map Φ \Phi on 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) {\cal T}({\cal H}) can be represented by means of a non unique set of Kraus operators { K j } j ∈ J \{K_{j}\}_{j\in J} , K j ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) K_{j}\in{\cal B}({\cal H}) , with index set J J at most countable, as follows: Φ ⁡ ( A ) = ∑ j ∈ J K j ​ A ​ K j ∗ , with ​ ∑ j ∈ J K j ∗ ​ K j = 𝕀 , \Phi(A)=\sum_{j\in J}K_{j}AK_{j}^{*},\ \ \text{with }\ \sum_{j\in J}K_{j}^{*}K_{j}={\mathbb{I}}, (4.1) where the convergence of the second sum is understood in the strong sense, implying the first sum converges in the trace norm sense. Conversely, any map Φ \Phi defined by ( ) yields a CPTP map on 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) {\cal T}({\cal H}) . Moreover such maps have operator norms equal to one: sup A ∈ 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) A ≠ 0 ‖ Φ ⁡ ( A ) ‖ 1 ‖ A ‖ 1 = 1 . \sup_{A\in{\cal T}({\cal H})\atop A\neq 0}\frac{\|\Phi(A)\|_{1}}{\|A\|_{1}}=1. (4.2) If dim ℋ < ∞ \dim{\cal H}<\infty , the index set J J can be chosen so that | J | ≤ ( dim ℋ ) 2 |J|\leq(\dim{\cal H})^{2} . In this case, CPTP maps can be unital since 𝕀 ∈ 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) {\mathbb{I}}\in{\cal T}({\cal H}) ; consider the map defined by Φ ⁡ ( A ) = U ​ A ​ U ∗ \Phi(A)=UAU^{*} , where U U is a unitary operator on ℋ {\cal H} , for example. Furthermore, since ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) {\cal B}({\cal H}) is the dual of 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) {\cal T}({\cal H}) with duality bracket ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) × 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) ∋ ( B , A ) ↦ tr ⁡ ( B ​ A ) ∈ ℂ , {\cal B}({\cal H})\times{\cal T}({\cal H})\ni(B,A)\mapsto{\rm tr}(BA)\in{\mathbb{C}}, (4.3) any map Φ : 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) → 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) \Phi:{\cal T}({\cal H})\rightarrow{\cal T}({\cal H}) admits an adjoint Φ ∗ : ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) → ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) \Phi^{*}:{\cal B}({\cal H})\rightarrow{\cal B}({\cal H}) defined by tr ⁡ ( B ​ Φ ​ ( A ) ) = tr ⁡ ( Φ ∗ ​ ( B ) ​ A ) , ∀ ( B , A ) ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) × 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) . {\rm tr}(B\Phi(A))={\rm tr}(\Phi^{*}(B)A),\ \ \forall\ (B,A)\in{\cal B}({\cal H})\times{\cal T}({\cal H}). (4.4) By Kraus’s Theorem, one also gets that the adjoint of a CPTP map given by ( ) has the form Φ ∗ ​ ( B ) = ∑ j ∈ J K j ∗ ​ B ​ K j , with ​ ∑ j ∈ J K j ∗ ​ K j = 𝕀 , \Phi^{*}(B)=\sum_{j\in J}K_{j}^{*}BK_{j},\ \ \text{with }\ \sum_{j\in J}K_{j}^{*}K_{j}={\mathbb{I}}, (4.5) where the first sum converges in the strong sense, a consequence of the second one converging in the strong sense. The map Φ ∗ \Phi^{*} is CP and unital and, moreover, any CP unital map on ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) {\cal B}({\cal H}) has the form ( ). Also Φ ∗ \Phi^{*} has operator norm equal to one sup B ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( ℋ ) B ≠ 0 ‖ Φ ∗ ​ ( B ) ‖ ‖ B ‖ = 1 . \sup_{B\in{\cal B}({\cal H})\atop B\neq 0}\frac{\|\Phi^{*}(B)\|}{\|B\|}=1. (4.6) We finally introduce the set of states or density matrices, a convex subset of 𝒯 ⁡ ( ℋ ) {\cal T}({\cal H}) , by 𝒟ℳ ( ℋ ) = { ρ ∈ 𝒯 ( ℋ ) , s . t . ρ = ρ ∗ ≥ 0 and tr ( ρ ) = 1 } , {\cal D}{\cal M}({\cal H})=\{\rho\in{\cal T}({\cal H}),\ {\rm s.t.}\ \rho=\rho^{*}\geq 0\ {\rm and}\ {\rm tr}(\rho)=1\}, (4.7) which is invariant under CPTP maps. The essential part of the dynamics is thus driven by Φ Diag \Phi^{\rm Diag} and takes place inside Diag ​ 𝒯 ​ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) {\rm Diag}{\cal T}(l^{2}(D)) . ii) The extension of Φ Diag \Phi^{\rm Diag} to ℬ ​ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) {\cal B}(l^{2}(D)) reads Φ Diag ​ ( ⋅ ) = ∑ x ∈ V ∑ y ∼ x z ∼ x | S z ​ y ​ ( x ) | 2 ​ | z ​ x ⟩ ​ ⟨ x ​ y | ⋅ | x ​ y ⟩ ​ ⟨ z ​ x | \Phi^{\rm Diag}(\cdot)=\sum_{x\in V}\sum_{y\sim x\atop z\sim x}|S_{zy}(x)|^{2}|zx\rangle\langle xy|\cdot|xy\rangle\langle zx| (4.21) with Kraus operators K z ​ y ​ ( x ) = S z ​ y ​ ( x ) ​ | z ​ x ⟩ ​ ⟨ x ​ y | K_{zy}(x)=S_{zy}(x)|zx\rangle\langle xy| mapping ℋ x I {\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I} to ℋ x O {\cal H}_{x}^{\rm O} and satisfying ∑ x ∈ V ∑ y ∼ x z ∼ x K z ​ y ∗ ​ ( x ) ​ K z ​ y ​ ( x ) = ∑ x ∈ V P x I = 𝕀 , ∑ x ∈ V ∑ y ∼ x z ∼ x K z ​ y ​ ( x ) ​ K z ​ y ∗ ​ ( x ) = ∑ x ∈ V P x O = 𝕀 , \sum_{x\in V}\sum_{y\sim x\atop z\sim x}K_{zy}^{*}(x)K_{zy}(x)=\sum_{x\in V}P_{x}^{\rm I}={\mathbb{I}},\ \ \ \sum_{x\in V}\sum_{y\sim x\atop z\sim x}K_{zy}(x)K^{*}_{zy}(x)=\sum_{x\in V}P_{x}^{\rm O}={\mathbb{I}}, (4.22) with convergence in the strong sense. When restricted to 𝒟ℳ ​ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) {\cal D}{\cal M}(l^{2}(D)) , the set of density matrices, the CPTP map Φ 𝒮 \Phi_{{\cal S}} describes the one time step quantum evolution of a quantum walker in a mixed state, now characterized by a density matrix. Again, the dynamical system ( Φ 𝒮 n ) n ∈ ℕ (\Phi_{{\cal S}}^{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}} on 𝒯 ​ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) {\cal T}(l^{2}(D)) or 𝒟ℳ ​ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) {\cal D}{\cal M}(l^{2}(D)) , and the related spectral properties of the CPTP map Φ 𝒮 ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( 𝒯 ⁡ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) ) \Phi_{{\cal S}}\in{\cal B}({\cal T}(l^{2}(D))) are of interest. We will address the spectrum of Φ 𝒮 \Phi_{{\cal S}} , making use of the specifics of our construction. Quantum Mechanical Interpretation: Similarly to the case of pure states considered in Section , quantum mechanical considerations induce natural time dependent distribution probabilities in the framework of density matrices. Given an initial state ρ 0 ∈ 𝒟ℳ ⁡ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) \rho_{0}\in{\cal D}{\cal M}(l^{2}(D)) , the state at time n n , is ρ n = Φ 𝒮 n ​ ( ρ 0 ) ∈ 𝒟ℳ ⁡ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) \rho_{n}=\Phi_{{\cal S}}^{n}(\rho_{0})\in{\cal D}{\cal M}(l^{2}(D)) . The quantum mechanical probability to find the corresponding quantum walker at time n n on the directed edge ( x ​ y ) (xy) of G G , by a measurement of its position, is ℙ n ρ 0 ​ ( x ​ y ) = tr ⁡ ( | x ​ y ⟩ ​ ⟨ x ​ y | ​ Φ 𝒮 n ​ ( ρ 0 ) ) = tr ⁡ ( | x ​ y ⟩ ​ ⟨ x ​ y | ​ ρ n ) = ⟨ x ​ y | ρ n ​ x ​ y ⟩ . {\mathbb{P}}_{n}^{\rho_{0}}(xy)={\rm tr}(|xy\rangle\langle xy|\Phi_{{\cal S}}^{n}(\rho_{0}))={\rm tr}(|xy\rangle\langle xy|\rho_{n})=\langle xy|\rho_{n}xy\rangle. (4.23) The probability to find the quantum walker at time n ∈ ℕ n\in{\mathbb{N}} in the subspace ℋ x I {\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I} or on the vertex x ∈ V x\in V , by a measurement of its position, is given by ℚ n ρ 0 ​ ( x ) = tr ⁡ ( P x I ​ Φ 𝒮 n ​ ( ρ 0 ) ) = tr ⁡ ( P x I ​ ρ n ) = ∑ y ∼ x ℙ n ρ 0 ​ ( x ​ y ) . {\mathbb{Q}}_{n}^{\rho_{0}}(x)={\rm tr}(P_{x}^{\rm I}\Phi_{{\cal S}}^{n}(\rho_{0}))={\rm tr}(P_{x}^{\rm I}\rho_{n})=\sum_{y\sim x}{\mathbb{P}}_{n}^{\rho_{0}}(xy). (4.24) Proposition yields the following operational interpretation of the quantum channel defined by Φ 𝒮 \Phi_{{\cal S}} . Given an initial quantum state ρ 0 ∈ 𝒟ℳ ⁡ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) \rho_{0}\in{\cal D}{\cal M}(l^{2}(D)) , with ρ 0 > 0 \rho_{0}>0 , one measures the position of the quantum walker on the vertices V V . The probability to get the outcome x ∈ V x\in V by this measurement is ℚ 0 ρ 0 ​ ( x ) = tr ⁡ ( P x I ​ ρ 0 ) > 0 {\mathbb{Q}}_{0}^{\rho_{0}}(x)={\rm tr}(P_{x}^{\rm I}\rho_{0})>0 and the wave packet reduction postulate says that after getting the outcome x x in the measurement process, the state immediately becomes P x I ​ ρ 0 ​ P x I / tr ⁡ ( P x I ​ ρ 0 ) P_{x}^{\rm I}\rho_{0}P_{x}^{\rm I}/{\rm tr}(P_{x}^{\rm I}\rho_{0}) . The expectation value ρ ¯ \bar{\rho} of the state obtained after a position measurement is then ρ ¯ = ∑ x ∈ V P x I ​ ρ 0 ​ P x I tr ⁡ ( P x I ​ ρ 0 ) ​ ℚ 0 ρ 0 ​ ( x ) = ∑ x ∈ V P x I ​ ρ 0 ​ P x I = D I ​ ( ρ 0 ) , \bar{\rho}=\sum_{x\in V}\frac{P_{x}^{\rm I}\rho_{0}P_{x}^{\rm I}}{{\rm tr}(P_{x}^{\rm I}\rho_{0})}{\mathbb{Q}}_{0}^{\rho_{0}}(x)=\sum_{x\in V}P_{x}^{\rm I}\rho_{0}P_{x}^{\rm I}=D^{\rm I}(\rho_{0}), (4.25) inducing decoherence. Hence, the action of the quantum channel Φ 𝒮 \Phi_{{\cal S}} on the state ρ 0 \rho_{0} results in first taking its expectation value with respect to a position measurement, so that the state becomes D I ​ ( ρ 0 ) D^{\rm I}(\rho_{0}) , followed by evolving the result by means of the unitary quantum channel U 𝒮 ⋅ U 𝒮 ∗ U_{\cal S}\cdot U_{\cal S}{}^{*} . 4.2 Quantum Trajectory We strengthen this interpretation by considering the Quantum Trajectory associated with the measurement of the position on V V . See [ H ] for quantum measurement theory, [ KM1 , KM2 ] for the first analyses of quantum trajectories and [ BJPP ] for a recent account of this topic. The iterative protocol is as follows (dropping unessential indices from the notation and writing ℋ {\cal H} for l 2 ​ ( D ) l^{2}(D) ). • At time 0 0 , the state is ρ 0 ∈ 𝒟ℳ ⁡ ( ℋ ) \rho_{0}\in{\cal D}{\cal M}({\cal H}) . We measure the position on V V and get an outcome x 1 ∈ V x_{1}\in V , with probability tr ⁡ ( P x 1 ​ ρ 0 ) {\rm tr}(P_{x_{1}}\rho_{0}) and evolve the reduced state by U ⋅ U ∗ U\cdot U^{*} to get at time 1 1 ρ 1 = U ​ P x 1 ​ ρ 0 ​ P x 1 ​ U ∗ / tr ⁡ ( P x 1 ​ ρ 0 ) . \rho_{1}=UP_{x_{1}}\rho_{0}P_{x_{1}}U^{*}/{\rm tr}(P_{x_{1}}\rho_{0}). (4.26) Denoting ℙ 1 ​ ( x 1 ) = tr ⁡ ( P x 1 ​ ρ 0 ) {\mathbb{P}}_{1}(x_{1})={\rm tr}(P_{x_{1}}\rho_{0}) the probability to get the outcome x 1 ∈ V x_{1}\in V , we get ρ 1 ​ ℙ 1 ​ ( x 1 ) = U ​ P x 1 ​ ρ 0 ​ P x 1 ​ U ∗ . \rho_{1}{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(x_{1})=UP_{x_{1}}\rho_{0}P_{x_{1}}U^{*}. (4.27) • At time 1 1 , the state is ρ 1 ∈ 𝒟ℳ ⁡ ( ℋ ) \rho_{1}\in{\cal D}{\cal M}({\cal H}) . (4.31) • At time j j , j ≥ 1 j\geq 1 , the state is ρ j ∈ 𝒟ℳ ⁡ ( ℋ ) \rho_{j}\in{\cal D}{\cal M}({\cal H}) . Let us finally show that open SQWs are distinct from the open QWs devised in [ APSS1 ] . To do so, we need to consider regular graphs G G with d x = d d_{x}=d for all x ∈ V x\in V , which we further assume be finite, for simplicity. We have the identification l 2 ​ ( D ) = ⨁ x ∈ V ℋ x I ≃ l 2 ​ ( V ) ⊗ ℂ d l^{2}(D)=\bigoplus_{x\in V}{\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I}\simeq l^{2}(V)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{d} . Let { | τ ⟩ } τ ∈ I \{|\tau\rangle\}_{\tau\in I} with | I | = d |I|=d , be an ONB of ℋ x I ≃ ℂ d {\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I}\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{d} , and consider a labelling of the vertices so that the following identification holds, for each x ∈ V x\in V fixed, | x ​ y ⟩ ≃ | x ⟩ ⊗ | τ ⟩ , where y ∼ x , τ ∈ I . |xy\rangle\simeq|x\rangle\otimes|\tau\rangle,\ \ \text{where}\ \ {y\sim x},\ {\tau\in I}. (4.43) Hence the identification of basis vectors of ℬ ⁡ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) ≃ ℬ ⁡ ( l 2 ​ ( V ) ⊗ ℂ d ) {\cal B}(l^{2}(D))\simeq{\cal B}(l^{2}(V)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{d}) | x ​ y ⟩ ​ ⟨ x ′ ​ y ′ | ≃ | x ⟩ ​ ⟨ x ′ | ⊗ | τ ⟩ ​ ⟨ τ ′ | . |xy\rangle\langle x^{\prime}y^{\prime}|\simeq|x\rangle\langle x^{\prime}|\otimes|\tau\rangle\langle\tau^{\prime}|. (4.44) The open QWs introduced in [ APSS1 ] are denoted by ℳ {\cal M} , and act on density matrices on l 2 ​ ( V ) ⊗ ℂ d l^{2}(V)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{d} . Their defining characteristics is that they leave the following subset of states invariant 𝒱 = { ρ ∈ 𝒟ℳ ( l 2 ( V ) ⊗ ℂ d ) | ρ = ∑ x ∈ V | x ⟩ ⟨ x | ⊗ ρ ( x ) , ρ ( x ) ∈ ℬ ( ℂ d ) } , {\cal V}=\Big\{\rho\in{\cal D}{\cal M}(l^{2}(V)\otimes{\mathbb{C}}^{d})\ |\ \rho=\sum_{x\in V}|x\rangle\langle x|\otimes\rho(x),\ \rho(x)\in{\cal B}({\mathbb{C}}^{d})\Big\}, (4.45) see Corollary 2.5 in [ APSS1 ] . Now, | x ​ y ⟩ ​ ⟨ x ​ y | ≃ | x ⟩ ​ ⟨ x | ⊗ | τ ⟩ ​ ⟨ τ | ∈ 𝒱 |xy\rangle\langle xy|\simeq|x\rangle\langle x|\otimes|\tau\rangle\langle\tau|\in{\cal V} , while the computation Φ 𝒮 ​ ( | x ​ y ⟩ ​ ⟨ x ​ y | ) = ∑ z ∼ x t ∼ x S z ​ y ​ ( x ) ​ S t ​ y ​ ( x ) ¯ ​ | z ​ x ⟩ ​ ⟨ t ​ x | \Phi_{{\cal S}}(|xy\rangle\langle xy|)=\sum_{z\sim x\atop t\sim x}S_{zy}(x)\overline{S_{ty}(x)}|zx\rangle\langle tx| (4.46) shows Φ 𝒮 ​ ( | x ​ y ⟩ ​ ⟨ x ​ y | ) \Phi_{{\cal S}}(|xy\rangle\langle xy|) is not equivalent to an element of 𝒱 {\cal V} , unless S ⁡ ( x ) S(x) is a permutation matrix times a diagonal matrix of phases. We now turn to the spectral properties of Φ 𝒮 \Phi_{{\cal S}} . 5 Spectral and Dynamical Properties of Φ 𝒮 \Phi_{{\cal S}} We will mainly consider the finite dimensional case corresponding to finite graphs G G in this section, and will comment along the way on the infinite graph case. For G G finite, we will not distinguish 𝒯 ​ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) {\cal T}(l^{2}(D)) and ℬ ​ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) {\cal B}(l^{2}(D)) . Let us equip ℬ ​ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) {\cal B}(l^{2}(D)) with the Hilbert-Schmidt scalar product to make it a Hilbert space, ⟨ A , B ⟩ H ​ S = tr ⁡ ( A ∗ ​ B ) , ∀ A , B ∈ ℬ ⁡ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) , s.t. ‖ A ‖ H ​ S = tr ⁡ ( A ∗ ​ A ) . \langle A,B\rangle_{HS}={\rm tr}(A^{*}B),\ \ \forall A,B\in{\cal B}(l^{2}(D)),\ \ \text{s.t.}\ \ \|A\|_{HS}=\sqrt{{\rm tr}(A^{*}A)}. (5.1) We denote the adjoint of a map Φ ∈ ℬ ​ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) \Phi\in{\cal B}(l^{2}(D)) with respect to this scalar product by Φ † \Phi^{\dagger} . In particular, we have Φ 𝒮 ( ⋅ ) † = ∑ x ∈ V K ∗ ( x ) ⋅ K ( x ) , ( U 𝒮 ⋅ U 𝒮 ) ∗ † = U 𝒮 ⋅ ∗ U 𝒮 , D # = D # , † # ∈ { O , I } , \Phi_{{\cal S}}{}^{\dagger}(\cdot)=\sum_{x\in V}K^{*}(x)\cdot K(x),\ \ (U_{\cal S}\cdot U_{\cal S}{}^{*})^{\dagger}=U_{\cal S}{}^{*}\cdot U_{\cal S},\ \ D^{\#}=D^{\#}{}^{\dagger},\ \ \#\in\{{\rm O,I}\}, (5.2) which makes D # D^{\#} and Diag , recall ( ), orthogonal projectors on ℬ ​ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) {\cal B}(l^{2}(D)) and implies Φ 𝒮 † = D I ∘ Φ 𝒮 † ∘ D O . \Phi_{{\cal S}}{}^{\dagger}=D^{\rm I}\circ\Phi_{{\cal S}}{}^{\dagger}\circ D^{\rm O}. Theorem 5.1 Let G G be finite. The main spectral properties of Φ Diag \Phi^{\rm Diag} , determining σ ⁡ ( Φ 𝒮 ) ∖ { 0 } \sigma(\Phi_{{\cal S}})\setminus\{0\} , read as follows according to Perron-Frobenius Theorem, see e.g. [ KS , N , Wo ] : • σ ( Φ Diag ) ⊂ { z ∈ ℂ , s . t . | z | ≤ 1 } \sigma(\Phi^{\rm Diag})\subset\{z\in{\mathbb{C}},\ {\rm s.t.}\ |z|\leq 1\} , with semi-simple modulus one eigenvalues. • If Φ Diag \Phi^{\rm Diag} is irreducible, 1 1 is a simple eigenvalue with corresponding eigenvector ∑ x ∼ y | x ​ y ⟩ ​ ⟨ x ​ y | \sum_{x\sim y}|xy\rangle\langle xy| . Moreover, the whole spectrum is invariant under rotation by a the angle 2 ​ π / p 2\pi/p . • If Φ Diag \Phi^{\rm Diag} is irreducible and aperiodic, 1 1 is the only eigenvalue of modulus one and is simple. In particular, we have Proposition 5.5 Assume S z ​ y ​ ( x ) ≠ 0 S_{zy}(x)\neq 0 , for all x , y , z ∈ V x,y,z\in V , y ∼ x , z ∼ x y\sim x,z\sim x . Then the bistochastic map Φ Diag \Phi^{\rm Diag} is irreducible. Moreover, Φ Diag \Phi^{\rm Diag} is aperiodic if the graph G G contains a cycle with an odd number of vertices, and it is irreducible with period 2 otherwise. Remark 5.6 Irreducibility may hold under weaker assumptions on the scattering matrices, see the example of the tree with three vertices discussed in Section . Proof : Let Γ \Gamma be the graph associated with the matrix Φ Diag \Phi^{\rm Diag} as follows. It has vertices labelled by the directed edges of G G , ( x ​ y ) (xy) , x ∼ y x\sim y , and directed edges from ( x ​ y ) (xy) to ( z ​ t ) (zt) iff Φ z ​ t ​ x ​ y Diag = δ x ​ t ​ | S z ​ y ​ ( x ) | 2 > 0 \Phi^{\rm Diag}_{zt\,xy}=\delta_{xt}|S_{zy}(x)|^{2}>0 . Under the assumption on the elements of S ⁡ ( x ) S(x) , we get that Γ \Gamma has an edge from ( x ​ y ) (xy) to ( z ​ x ) (zx) for all z ∼ x z\sim x , y ∼ x y\sim x , x ∈ V x\in V , i.e. between any incoming edge of G G at x x and any outgoing edge of G G from x x . The matrix Φ Diag \Phi^{\rm Diag} is irreducible iff there exists a path of directed edges of Γ \Gamma between any two of its vertices ( x ​ y ) (xy) and ( z ​ t ) (zt) . Since G G is connected, there exists a path from x x to t t , made of directed edges ( x 1 ​ x ) (x_{1}x) , ( x 2 ​ x 1 ) (x_{2}x_{1}) , …, ( t ​ x n ) (tx_{n}) . But ( x ​ y ) (xy) and ( x 1 ​ x ) (x_{1}x) are connected in Γ \Gamma , as are ( x 1 ​ x ) (x_{1}x) and ( x 2 ​ x 1 ) (x_{2}x_{1}) , up to ( x n ​ x n − 1 ) (x_{n}x_{n-1}) and ( t ​ x n ) (tx_{n}) , so that Φ Diag \Phi^{\rm Diag} is irreducible. Because for any directed edge ( x ​ y ) (xy) of G G , ( x ​ y ) (xy) and ( y ​ x ) (yx) form a directed edge of Γ \Gamma , the matrix element ( Φ Diag 2 ) x ​ y ​ x ​ y > 0 ({\Phi^{\rm Diag}}^{2})_{xy\,xy}>0 , so that either the irreducible matrix Φ Diag \Phi^{\rm Diag} has period 2, or it is aperiodic. When G G contains a cycle with n n vertices, for any edge ( x ​ y ) (xy) of G G between vertices of the cycle, we have ( Φ Diag n ) x ​ y ​ x ​ y > 0 ({\Phi^{\rm Diag}}^{n})_{xy\,xy}>0 , estimating the matrix element by the product of the elements along consecutive edges. Since an edge ( x ​ y ) (xy) satisfies ( Φ Diag 2 ​ p + 1 ) x ​ y ​ x ​ y > 0 ({\Phi^{\rm Diag}}^{2p+1})_{xy\,xy}>0 , p ∈ N ∗ p\in N^{*} , iff G G contains a cycle with 2 ​ p + 1 2p+1 vertices including x x and y y , this yields the result. □ \Box Consequently, the discrete asymptotic dynamics induced by Φ 𝒮 \Phi_{{\cal S}} is essentially independent of the set of scattering matrices 𝒮 {\cal S} that parameterize Φ 𝒮 \Phi_{{\cal S}} : Corollary 5.7 Let Φ 𝒮 \Phi_{{\cal S}} be defined on ℬ ​ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) {\cal B}(l^{2}(D)) with | V | < ∞ |V|<\infty . Hence, for infinite graphs, the dynamics can send the state at infinity, preventing the existence of an asymptotic state. 6 Induced Open Quantum Walks on G G The open QW Φ 𝒮 ∈ 𝒯 ⁡ ( l 2 ​ ( D ) ) \Phi_{{\cal S}}\in{\cal T}(l^{2}(D)) defined above lives on the directed edges of G G . P x ​ y = | ⟨ y | χ ⁡ ( x ) ⟩ | 2 ≥ 0 , for ​ x , y ∈ V . P_{xy}=|\langle y|\chi(x)\rangle|^{2}\geq 0,\ \text{for}\ x,y\in V. The matrix P P is the transition matrix of a discrete time Markov chain M M on V V , M : ℕ → V M:{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow V such that at time t ∈ ℕ t\in{\mathbb{N}} and for x , y ∈ V x,y\in V , ℙ ⁡ ( M ⁡ ( t ) = y | M ⁡ ( t − 1 ) = x ) = | ⟨ y ⁡ | χ ⁡ ( x ) ⟩ | 2 = P x ​ y . {\mathbb{P}}(M(t)=y|M(t-1)=x)=|\langle y|\chi(x)\rangle|^{2}=P_{xy}. (6.19) A probability vector on V V is a row vector of the form p = ( p x ) x ∈ V p=(p_{x})_{x\in V} , i.e. , given an ordering of the vertices, p = ( p x 1 , p x 2 , … , p x | V | ) , p=(p_{x_{1}},p_{x_{2}},\dots,p_{x_{|V|}}), (6.20) with p x ≥ 0 p_{x}\geq 0 and ∑ x ∈ V p x = 1 \sum_{x\in V}p_{x}=1 . The support of p p is the set { x ∈ V s.t. p x > 0 } \{x\in V\ \text{s.t.}\ p_{x}>0\} , and p p is strictly positive if its support is V V . For a set of vertices S ⊂ V S\subset V , we denote the probability of this set by p ⁡ ( S ) = ∑ x ∈ S p x p(S)=\sum_{x\in S}p_{x} . Given an initial probability vector p p we have for all t ∈ ℕ t\in{\mathbb{N}} , ℙ ⁡ ( M ⁡ ( 0 ) = x ) = p x , ∀ x ∈ V , ⇒ ℙ p ​ ( M ⁡ ( t ) = x ) = ( p ​ P t ) x , {\mathbb{P}}(M(0)=x)=p_{x}\ ,\forall x\in V,\ \Rightarrow\ {\mathbb{P}}_{p}(M(t)=x)=(pP^{t})_{x}, (6.21) where the subscript p p indicates the initial distribution of the Markov chain M M , see [ N , KS , Wo ] for example. In this case, we can deduce the large n n behaviour of the map Ψ 𝒮 n ​ ( ⋅ ) \Psi_{{\cal S}}^{n}(\cdot) from the properties of the stochastic matrix P P . Corollary 6.4 Let G G be finite. If P P is irreducible, there exists a strictly positive probability vector π = ( π x 1 , π x 2 , ⋯ , π x | V | ) \pi=(\pi_{x_{1}},\pi_{x_{2}},\cdots,\pi_{x_{|V|}}) such that with 𝟏 T = ( 1 , 1 ⋯ , 1 ) {\bf 1}^{T}=(1,1\cdots,1) and for N N large enough 1 N ​ ∑ n = 0 N − 1 P n = 𝟏 ​ π + O ⁡ ( N − 1 ) \frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}P^{n}={\bf 1}\pi+O(N^{-1}) and 1 N ​ ∑ n = 0 N − 1 Ψ 𝒮 n ​ ( ⋅ ) = ∑ y ∈ V | χ ⁡ ( y ) ⟩ ​ ⟨ χ ⁡ ( y ) | ​ π y ​ tr ​ ( ⋅ ) + O ⁡ ( N − 1 ) . \frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\Psi_{{\cal S}}^{n}(\cdot)=\sum_{y\in V}|\chi(y)\rangle\langle\chi(y)|\,\pi_{y}\,{\rm tr}(\cdot)+O(N^{-1}). (6.34) Proof : If P P is irreducible, Perron-Frobenius Theorem ensures the existence of a strictly positive probability row vector π = ( π x 1 , π x 2 , ⋯ , π x | V | ) \pi=(\pi_{x_{1}},\pi_{x_{2}},\cdots,\pi_{x_{|V|}}) such that with 𝟏 T = ( 1 , 1 ⋯ , 1 ) {\bf 1}^{T}=(1,1\cdots,1) and for N N large enough 1 N ​ ∑ n = 0 N − 1 P n = 𝟏 ​ π + O ⁡ ( N − 1 ) , \frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}P^{n}={\bf 1}\pi+O(N^{-1}), (6.35) see e.g. [ N , KS , Wo ] . Formula ( ) is proven similarly in the irreducible and aperiodic case. □ \Box Remark 6.5 i) Up to the strict positivity of π \pi , the Corollary can be given a purely spectral proof that we sketch in Appendix C for completeness. ii) Since the asymptotic map typically differs from 𝕀 V ​ tr ​ ( ⋅ ) / | V | {\mathbb{I}}_{V}{\rm tr}(\cdot)/|V| , this shows that Ψ 𝒮 \Psi_{\cal S} is not entropy non-decreasing. iii) If P P fails to be irreducible, the limiting behaviours of Ψ 𝒮 ​ ( ρ 0 ) \Psi_{{\cal S}}(\rho_{0}) and ℚ n ρ 0 {\mathbb{Q}}_{n}^{\rho_{0}} depend on the initial state ρ 0 ∈ 𝒟ℳ ⁡ ( l 2 ​ ( V ) ) \rho_{0}\in{\cal D}{\cal M}(l^{2}(V)) , as shown on the example in Section . iv) The formula of the asymptotic state is likely to hold true for infinite graphs. We show that this is the case on the example of the next section. The results presented in Theorem and Corollary only depend on the form ( ) of the rank one Kraus operators of Ψ 𝒮 \Psi_{{\cal S}} . In particular, the asymptotic state of Ψ 𝒮 n \Psi_{{\cal S}}^{n} and corresponding probabilities to find the walker on each vertex are monitored by the invariant probability vector π \pi of the Markov chain associated with P P . This is to be contrasted with the corresponding asymptotic quantities stated in Corollary and ( ) for the open QW Φ 𝒮 n \Phi_{{\cal S}}^{n} which only depend on the degrees of the vertices of G G . The non trivial dependence of the invariant probability vector π \pi on the parameters of the construction, i.e. the graph G G , the scattering matrices 𝒮 = { S ⁡ ( x ) } x ∈ V {\cal S}=\{S(x)\}_{x\in V} and the vectors { ω ⁡ ( x ) } x ∈ V \{\omega(x)\}_{x\in V} appearing in R R , is displayed by the examples of the next section. 6.1 Examples We illustrate the variety of behaviours induced open SQWs on graphs can display as a function of the scattering matrices by working out the cases where they correspond to the α − \alpha- Grover Walk, and to the Discrete Fourier Transform. Let G G be an arbitrary finite graph and consider the boundary operator R R and its adjoint ( 6.1 ) parameterized by the family of unit vectors ω ⁡ ( x ) = 𝟏 d x ∈ ℂ d x , ∀ x ∈ V . \omega(x)=\frac{\bf 1}{\sqrt{d_{x}}}\in{\mathbb{C}}^{d_{x}},\ \ \forall x\in V. (6.37) This choice is motivated by the fact that it yields an equal weight to each of the vectors spanning ℋ x I {\cal H}_{x}^{\rm I} , and thus is likely to make P P irreducible. The components of the vector v ⁡ ( x ) v(x) ( ) thus satisfy v z ​ ( x ) = 1 d x ​ ∑ y ∼ x S z ​ y ​ ( x ) = : 1 d x ​ L z ​ ( x ) , ∀ x ∈ V , z ∼ x . v_{z}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{x}}}\sum_{y\sim x}S_{zy}(x)=:\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{x}}}L_{z}(x),\ \ \forall x\in V,z\sim x. (6.40) For this choice of boundary operator, we now consider two families of scattering matrices 𝒮 = { S ⁡ ( x ) } x ∈ V . {\cal S}=\{S(x)\}_{x\in V}. 6.1.1 The α − \alpha- Grover Scattering Matrices Following ( ), we set ∀ x ∈ V \forall x\in V , S ⁡ ( x ) = 1 d x ​ | 𝟏 ⟩ ​ ⟨ 𝟏 | + e i ​ α ​ ( 𝕀 d x − 1 d x ​ | 𝟏 ⟩ ​ ⟨ 𝟏 | ) , S(x)=\frac{1}{d_{x}}|{\bf 1}\rangle\langle{\bf 1}|+e^{i\alpha}\big({\mathbb{I}}_{d_{x}}-\frac{1}{d_{x}}|{\bf 1}\rangle\langle{\bf 1}|\big), (6.41) the matrix elements of which read, with ν ⁡ ( α ) = 1 − e i ​ α ≠ 0 \nu(\alpha)=1-e^{i\alpha}\neq 0 , S z ​ y ​ ( x ) = { e i ​ α + ν ⁡ ( α ) d x if ​ y = z ∼ x ν ⁡ ( α ) d x if ​ y ≠ z , y ∼ x , z ∼ x 0 otherwise. Since G G is connected, P P is irreducible. Here, the j ​ k jk matrix element of S ⁡ ( x ) S(x) corresponds to S x j ​ x k ​ ( x ) S_{x_{j}x_{k}}(x) , for a labelling of the d x d_{x} adjacent vertices x 1 , x 2 , … , x d x x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{d_{x}} of x x . Since the rows of a unitary matrix are orthogonal, we get for x j ∼ x x_{j}\sim x , L x j ​ ( x ) = { d x if ​ j = 1 , 0 if ​ j ≥ 2 , L_{x_{j}}(x)=\begin{cases}\sqrt{d_{x}}&\text{if}\ j=1,\\ 0&\text{if}\ j\geq 2,\end{cases} (6.48) so that θ ⁡ ( x ) = 1 d x 1 ​ | x 1 ⟩ \theta(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{x_{1}}}}|x_{1}\rangle , with x 1 ∼ x x_{1}\sim x . Consequently, ∀ x , y ∈ V \forall x,y\in V , P x ​ y = { 1 d x 1 if ​ y = x 1 ∼ x 1 − 1 d x 1 if ​ y = x 0 otherwise. P_{xy}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{d_{x_{1}}}&\text{if }y=x_{1}\sim x\\ 1-\frac{1}{d_{x_{1}}}&\text{if }y=x\\ 0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases} (6.49) Let us stress that P x ​ y P_{xy} and the induced dynamics depend on the choice of the vertices labelling. This stochastic matrix is in general not irreducible, but its invariant probability vectors can be determined. Once the labelling of the adjacent vertices to x x is fixed for all x ∈ V x\in V , P P defines the map N N on V V such that N : \displaystyle N: V → V \displaystyle\ V\rightarrow V x ↦ N ⁡ ( x ) = y , where ​ y ∼ x ​ is uniquely determined by ​ P x ​ y > 0 . \displaystyle\ x\mapsto N(x)=y,\ \ \text{where }\ y\sim x\text{ is uniquely determined by }P_{xy}>0. (6.50) We can then construct a directed subgraph Σ ⊂ G \Sigma\subset G (without loops) associated with P P or equivalently with N N : Σ \Sigma has same vertex set V V as G G and ( y ​ x ) (yx) form an edge of Σ \Sigma iff y = N ⁡ ( x ) y=N(x) . Σ \Sigma is also known as a functional graph or successor graph , and may or may not by connected. Consider the set of the connected components of Σ \Sigma , CC ( Σ ) = ∪ i = 1 r C i where C i ⊆ V is connected in Σ , i = 1 , … , r . {\rm CC}(\Sigma)=\cup_{i=1}^{r}C_{i}\ \ \text{where}\ \ C_{i}\subseteq V\ \text{is connected in }\Sigma,\ i=1,\dots,r. (6.51) Each component C i ∈ CC ⁡ ( Σ ) C_{i}\in{\rm CC}(\Sigma) , i = 1 , … , r i=1,\dots,r , consists of one cycle, the vertices of which are possibly the roots of a finite tree.
